People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I guess… I don’t feel like myself anymore. I mean, I came in because I’m just kind of flat and quiet most of the time now. I don’t talk to people much, even at work, and I used to at least say a few things, you know, about the floors or the weather or whatever.

And getting started on my cleaning route… it feels like pushing one of those loaded carts uphill. Those carts, if the wheel’s bad, they pull to one side—anyway, that’s what it feels like in me. Like everything takes too much just to begin.

Not really, no. I used to look forward to Saturdays because I’d go fishing, even if I didn’t catch anything—there was this one spot where the reeds always got tangled in the line, drove me crazy, but I liked being out there.

And I used to do crosswords with coffee, too. Morning paper, same mug… or at work I’d show the younger guys little tricks, like how to strip a floor without making a mess of the edges. Now I mostly just let all that sit. It doesn’t really give me anything back anymore.

I… yeah, I’ve had thoughts like that. Not like I’ve got some big plan written down or anything. It’s more that a lot of mornings I wake up and think, maybe it’d be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all.

Those carts, if the wheel’s bad, they pull to one side—anyway, that’s what it feels like in me. Like everything takes too much just to begin.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About a year and… five months, I think. It kind of started after I had to leave my old apartment, my old neighborhood. I still think about that place—the corner store had this bell on the door, real loud, you could hear it from the sidewalk sometimes. But yeah… since then it’s been building, not all at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities or hobbies that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I used to look forward to Saturdays because I’d go fishing, even if I didn’t catch anything—there was this one spot where the reeds always got tangled in the line, drove me crazy, but I liked being out there.\n\nAnd I used to do crosswords with coffee, too. Morning paper, same mug… or at work I’d show the younger guys little tricks, like how to strip a floor without making a mess of the edges. Now I mostly just let all that sit. It doesn’t really give me anything back anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I’ve had thoughts like that. Not like I’ve got some big plan written down or anything. It’s more that a lot of mornings I wake up and think, maybe it’d be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all.\n\nMornings are strange that way—the light comes in kind of gray through the blinds, and there’s that few seconds before you remember what day it is. I used to get right up because the building floors don’t clean themselves, you know… but now sometimes I just lie there thinking that. And it scares me enough that I guess I should say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge?\n\nPatient: Nervous or on edge… I’m not sure. I mean, I get uneasy sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s “often” like you mean.\n\nLike, the other day I kept hearing the radiator knock in the hallway—old buildings do that, especially when the heat kicks on—and I caught myself just sitting there listening to it longer than made sense. But I don’t know if that’s nerves or just… being worn out. So, yeah, I’m not really sure how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious… I don’t know. I mean, I know what people mean when they say it, like your stomach twists up or you’re waiting for bad news, but I don’t know if that’s what I’m having.\n\nSometimes I’m just sitting there and I feel kind of stirred up, but then I’ll notice the sink dripping or the hallway light buzzing, and I start thinking about how many times I used to change those bulbs at work because somebody always put in the cheap ones… so I don’t know. I haven’t really kept track of it as anxiety.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I don’t have some big plan, but a lot of mornings I wake up thinking it might be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all, and that scares me enough to say it out loud.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and depressive_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: When I was at my lowest—barely mind.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: There was a stretch of several weeks when my mood had lifted some and I was getting through meals and work a little better, but I still heard my name in the stairwell and stayed convinced the hallway cameras were being used to track me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My supervisor started writing up missed routes and repeated cleaning mistakes, and I stopped keeping up with church, fishing, and the people I used to check in on. eating, feeling guilty, and thinking I might not want to wake up—I was also hearing that man’s voice at work and believing people in the building were watching me.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n myself not getting back to the answer.\n- key: S022\n  name: Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior\n  description: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.\n  manifestation: There have been times at work when I keep wiping the same patch of glass over and over or stand in the supply closet holding the broom because it feels like I have to wait for the right sign before I can move on.\n- key: S023\n  name: Negative_Symptoms\n  description: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.\n  manifestation: What brought me in is I don’t feel like myself anymore—I barely talk to people, I just go flat and quiet, and even getting started on the cleaning route feels like pushing a loaded cart uphill.\n- key: S025\n  name: Loss_of_Interest\n  description: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.\n  manifestation: I used to look forward to fishing on Saturdays, doing crosswords with coffee, or showing the younger guys tricks for stripping a floor, but now I mostly let those things sit because they don’t give me anything back.\n- key: S026\n  name: Weight_Appetite_Change\n  description: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.\n  manifestation: Most days food just tastes like cardboard, so I skip meals or eat a few crackers after my shift, and my work pants have been hanging looser than they used to.\n- key: S029\n  name: Fatigue\n  description: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.\n  manifestation: Even if I sleep through the night, my arms and legs feel heavy by the time I get to the building, and small jobs like hauling trash bags wear me out more than they should.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep thinking I failed everybody by losing my old place and not keeping my life together, like if the church pantry is short on supplies or a hallway gets missed, somehow that’s on me too.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: I don’t have some big plan, but a lot of mornings I wake up thinking it might be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all, and that scares me enough to say it out loud.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and depressive_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: When I was at my lowest—barely eating, feeling guilty, and thinking I might not want to wake up—I was also hearing that man’s voice at work and believing people in the building were watching me.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n  name: Additional Requirement 2\n  description: depressive_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness (Criterion C)\n  manifestation: For most of the time since the move, the sadness, low energy, guilt, and loss of interest have been there in the background, even on days when the voices or camera worries were the main thing on my mind.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: There was a stretch of several weeks when my mood had lifted some and I was getting through meals and work a little better, but I still heard my name in the stairwell and stayed convinced the hallway cameras were being used to track me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My supervisor started writing up missed routes and repeated cleaning mistakes, and I stopped keeping up with church, fishing, and the people I used to check in on. I finally went to the employee assistance program because I could not keep my work routine straight anymore.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 5 months\n  manifestation: This has been building for about 1 year and 5 months, ever since I had to leave my old apartment and neighborhood.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 11 months\n  manifestation: The voices and worries about being watched have been going on for about 11 months now.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: The depression part has been with me for about 1 year and 2 months, starting not long after the move.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S023\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in your life where you felt completely back to normal after feeling depressed, before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to a time like that, where I was depressed and then, you know, completely normal again before this.\n\nI had days that were better than other days, sure. Like you get through a shift and the floors actually stay clean for five minutes, or the weather’s decent and you think maybe things aren’t so bad. But as far as a whole period where I was down like this and then it just cleared up? I don’t really remember that happening.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel completely better from a depressed period before this one started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in the way you’re saying, like a whole depressed period and then I was just completely fine after.\n\nI’ve had better days, sure. Sometimes a week goes smoother, you know, the trash rooms aren’t overflowing and nobody’s yelling about streaks on the lobby glass. That can make you feel like maybe you’re doing all right. But completely better from a depressed period before this one… I can’t really say that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it more than one, no. It doesn’t feel like separate chunks to me, like one ended and then another started.\n\nIt’s more like… when you mop a hallway and there’s still that dull patch that never really dries right, and you keep noticing it every time you walk by. I’ve had better days mixed in, but I can’t really sort it into “this was one depressed time” and “that was another.” It just feels like this same stretch, mostly.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you have any periods of feeling depressed that completely went away before this current episode started?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item.   },

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t feel like myself anymore. I mean, I came in because I’m just kind of flat and quiet most of the time now. I don’t talk to people much, even at work, and I used to at least say a few things, you know, about the floors or the weather or whatever.\n\nAnd getting started on my cleaning route… it feels like pushing one of those loaded carts uphill. Those carts, if the wheel’s bad, they pull to one side—anyway, that’s what it feels like in me. Like everything takes too much just to begin.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About a year and… five months, I think. It kind of started after I had to leave my old apartment, my old neighborhood. I still think about that place—the corner store had this bell on the door, real loud, you could hear it from the sidewalk sometimes. But yeah… since then it’s been building, not all at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities or hobbies that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I used to look forward to Saturdays because I’d go fishing, even if I didn’t catch anything—there was this one spot where the reeds always got tangled in the line, drove me crazy, but I liked being out there.\n\nAnd I used to do crosswords with coffee, too. Morning paper, same mug… or at work I’d show the younger guys little tricks, like how to strip a floor without making a mess of the edges. Now I mostly just let all that sit. It doesn’t really give me anything back anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I’ve had thoughts like that. Not like I’ve got some big plan written down or anything. It’s more that a lot of mornings I wake up and think, maybe it’d be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all.\n\nMornings are strange that way—the light comes in kind of gray through the blinds, and there’s that few seconds before you remember what day it is. I used to get right up because the building floors don’t clean themselves, you know… but now sometimes I just lie there thinking that. And it scares me enough that I guess I should say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge?\n\nPatient: Nervous or on edge… I’m not sure. I mean, I get uneasy sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s “often” like you mean.\n\nLike, the other day I kept hearing the radiator knock in the hallway—old buildings do that, especially when the heat kicks on—and I caught myself just sitting there listening to it longer than made sense. But I don’t know if that’s nerves or just… being worn out. So, yeah, I’m not really sure how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious… I don’t know. I mean, I know what people mean when they say it, like your stomach twists up or you’re waiting for bad news, but I don’t know if that’s what I’m having.\n\nSometimes I’m just sitting there and I feel kind of stirred up, but then I’ll notice the sink dripping or the hallway light buzzing, and I start thinking about how many times I used to change those bulbs at work because somebody always put in the cheap ones… so I don’t know. I haven’t really kept track of it as anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, but my work pants have been… kind of hanging off me more than they used to.\n\nFood just doesn’t taste right most days. Like cardboard, almost. I’ll make something and then just look at it, or I’ll skip it and end up eating a few crackers after my shift. Those vending machine crackers, the stale kind—though sometimes they’re the only thing that feels easy. So, yeah… I guess I’ve lost some weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling flat or depressed like this before the one that started after you left your old apartment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was like this before, not in the same way. The flat feeling… the low feeling… that really started not too long after I moved out of the old apartment.\n\nIt’s been going on a while now. I guess about a year and a couple months, if I count it out. Funny how you don’t notice the time passing until you think about rent dates and moving boxes and all that junk you never unpack. But yeah… the depressed part has been around since after the move, about a year and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or depressed before in your life, prior to the episode that started after you left your old apartment?\n\nPatient: Before that? I don’t really think so… not like this.\n\nI mean, I’ve had bad stretches, sure—everybody does. Work gets miserable, bills stack up, pipes burst in the winter and then you’re the one mopping it at 2 a.m.… but this low feeling, the one I’m talking about, that started after I left the old apartment.\n\nIt’s been about… a year and two months now, I think. Not right on moving day, but not long after. That’s the one that’s stuck around.\n\nDoctor: Have you had an episode like this before, where you felt flat and depressed for an extended period, that completely went away before this one started?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I had one that came and went before this. I mean, I’ve had rough times, sure, like when a building changes management and suddenly everybody wants the floors done a different way, and you’re supposed to just know it—but not that same flat, heavy kind of depressed feeling for a long stretch.\n\nThis one’s been with me since not long after I moved out of the old apartment. About a year and two months now, if I’m counting right. It didn’t really clear up and then come back.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in your life where you felt completely back to normal after feeling depressed, before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to a time like that, where I was depressed and then, you know, completely normal again before this.\n\nI had days that were better than other days, sure. Like you get through a shift and the floors actually stay clean for five minutes, or the weather’s decent and you think maybe things aren’t so bad. But as far as a whole period where I was down like this and then it just cleared up? I don’t really remember that happening.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel completely better from a depressed period before this one started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in the way you’re saying, like a whole depressed period and then I was just completely fine after.\n\nI’ve had better days, sure. Sometimes a week goes smoother, you know, the trash rooms aren’t overflowing and nobody’s yelling about streaks on the lobby glass. That can make you feel like maybe you’re doing all right. But completely better from a depressed period before this one… I can’t really say that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it more than one, no. It doesn’t feel like separate chunks to me, like one ended and then another started.\n\nIt’s more like… when you mop a hallway and there’s still that dull patch that never really dries right, and you keep noticing it every time you walk by. I’ve had better days mixed in, but I can’t really sort it into “this was one depressed time” and “that was another.” It just feels like this same stretch, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any periods of feeling depressed that completely went away before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’m trying to think of it the way you’re asking, like there was a depressed time and then it went away clean before now. I can’t really put my finger on anything like that.\n\nThere were better days, sure. Sometimes if the building was quiet and I got all my work done, I’d think, okay, maybe I’m doing alright. But then it’s like the same heaviness is still there underneath. So no, I don’t think I had a period where it completely went away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I used to look forward to Saturdays because I’d go fishing, even if I didn’t catch anything—there was this one spot where the reeds always got tangled in the line, drove me crazy, but I liked being out there.\n\nAnd I used to do crosswords with coffee, too. Morning paper, same mug… or at work I’d show the younger guys little tricks, like how to strip a floor without making a mess of the edges. Now I mostly just let all that sit. I mean, I know what people mean when they say it, like your stomach twists up or you’re waiting for bad news, but I don’t know if that’s what I’m having.\n\nSometimes I’m just sitting there and I feel kind of stirred up, but then I’ll notice the sink dripping or the hallway light buzzing, and I start thinking about how many times I used to change those bulbs at work because somebody always put in the cheap ones… so I don’t know. I haven’t really kept track of it as anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, but my work pants have been… kind of hanging off me more than they used to.\n\nFood just doesn’t taste right most days. Like cardboard, almost. I’ll make something and then just look at it, or I’ll skip it and end up eating a few crackers after my shift. Those vending machine crackers, the stale kind—though sometimes they’re the only thing that feels easy. So, yeah… I guess I’ve lost some weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling flat or depressed like this before the one that started after you left your old apartment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was like this before, not in the same way. The flat feeling… the low feeling… that really started not too long after I moved out of the old apartment.\n\nIt’s been going on a while now. I guess about a year and a couple months, if I count it out. Funny how you don’t notice the time passing until you think about rent dates and moving boxes and all that junk you never unpack. But yeah… the depressed part has been around since after the move, about a year and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or depressed before in your life, prior to the episode that started after you left your old apartment?\n\nPatient: Before that? I don’t really think so… not like this.\n\nI mean, I’ve had bad stretches, sure—everybody does. Work gets miserable, bills stack up, pipes burst in the winter and then you’re the one mopping it at 2 a.m.… but this low feeling, the one I’m talking about, that started after I left the old apartment.\n\nIt’s been about… a year and two months now, I think. Not right on moving day, but not long after. That’s the one that’s stuck around.\n\nDoctor: Have you had an episode like this before, where you felt flat and depressed for an extended period, that completely went away before this one started?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I had one that came and went before this. I mean, I’ve had rough times, sure, like when a building changes management and suddenly everybody wants the floors done a different way, and you’re supposed to just know it—but not that same flat, heavy kind of depressed feeling for a long stretch.\n\nThis one’s been with me since not long after I moved out of the old apartment. About a year and two months now, if I’m counting right. It didn’t really clear up and then come back.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in your life where you felt completely back to normal after feeling depressed, before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to a time like that, where I was depressed and then, you know, completely normal again before this.\n\nI had days that were better than other days, sure. Like you get through a shift and the floors actually stay clean for five minutes, or the weather’s decent and you think maybe things aren’t so bad. But as far as a whole period where I was down like this and then it just cleared up? I don’t really remember that happening.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel completely better from a depressed period before this one started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in the way you’re saying, like a whole depressed period and then I was just completely fine after.\n\nI’ve had better days, sure. Sometimes a week goes smoother, you know, the trash rooms aren’t overflowing and nobody’s yelling about streaks on the lobby glass. That can make you feel like maybe you’re doing all right. But completely better from a depressed period before this one… I can’t really say that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it more than one, no. It doesn’t feel like separate chunks to me, like one ended and then another started.\n\nIt’s more like… when you mop a hallway and there’s still that dull patch that never really dries right, and you keep noticing it every time you walk by. I’ve had better days mixed in, but I can’t really sort it into “this was one depressed time” and “that was another.” It just feels like this same stretch, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any periods of feeling depressed that completely went away before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’m trying to think of it the way you’re asking, like there was a depressed time and then it went away clean before now. I can’t really put my finger on anything like that.\n\nThere were better days, sure. Sometimes if the building was quiet and I got all my work done, I’d think, okay, maybe I’m doing alright. But then it’s like the same heaviness is still there underneath. So no, I don’t think I had a period where it completely went away.\n\nDoctor: Did you have an episode of depression that completely went away before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: No, not completely. It’s been more like the same thing carrying on.\n\nThe depression part… I’d say it’s been with me about a year and two months now, starting not long after the move. I remember because after moving, there were all these boxes and little things to fix, and I kept thinking once things settled down I’d feel normal again, but it didn’t really lift all the way. So no, I don’t think there was an episode that went away before this.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean, I know what people mean when they say it, like your stomach twists up or you’re waiting for bad news, but I don’t know if that’s what I’m having.\n\nSometimes I’m just sitting there and I feel kind of stirred up, but then I’ll notice the sink dripping or the hallway light buzzing, and I start thinking about how many times I used to change those bulbs at work because somebody always put in the cheap ones… so I don’t know. I haven’t really kept track of it as anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, but my work pants have been… kind of hanging off me more than they used to.\n\nFood just doesn’t taste right most days. Like cardboard, almost. I’ll make something and then just look at it, or I’ll skip it and end up eating a few crackers after my shift. Those vending machine crackers, the stale kind—though sometimes they’re the only thing that feels easy. So, yeah… I guess I’ve lost some weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling flat or depressed like this before the one that started after you left your old apartment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was like this before, not in the same way. The flat feeling… the low feeling… that really started not too long after I moved out of the old apartment.\n\nIt’s been going on a while now. I guess about a year and a couple months, if I count it out. Funny how you don’t notice the time passing until you think about rent dates and moving boxes and all that junk you never unpack. But yeah… the depressed part has been around since after the move, about a year and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or depressed before in your life, prior to the episode that started after you left your old apartment?\n\nPatient: Before that? I don’t really think so… not like this.\n\nI mean, I’ve had bad stretches, sure—everybody does. Work gets miserable, bills stack up, pipes burst in the winter and then you’re the one mopping it at 2 a.m.… but this low feeling, the one I’m talking about, that started after I left the old apartment.\n\nIt’s been about… a year and two months now, I think. Not right on moving day, but not long after. That’s the one that’s stuck around.\n\nDoctor: Have you had an episode like this before, where you felt flat and depressed for an extended period, that completely went away before this one started?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I had one that came and went before this. I mean, I’ve had rough times, sure, like when a building changes management and suddenly everybody wants the floors done a different way, and you’re supposed to just know it—but not that same flat, heavy kind of depressed feeling for a long stretch.\n\nThis one’s been with me since not long after I moved out of the old apartment. About a year and two months now, if I’m counting right. It didn’t really clear up and then come back.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in your life where you felt completely back to normal after feeling depressed, before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to a time like that, where I was depressed and then, you know, completely normal again before this.\n\nI had days that were better than other days, sure. Like you get through a shift and the floors actually stay clean for five minutes, or the weather’s decent and you think maybe things aren’t so bad. But as far as a whole period where I was down like this and then it just cleared up? I don’t really remember that happening.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel completely better from a depressed period before this one started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in the way you’re saying, like a whole depressed period and then I was just completely fine after.\n\nI’ve had better days, sure. Sometimes a week goes smoother, you know, the trash rooms aren’t overflowing and nobody’s yelling about streaks on the lobby glass. That can make you feel like maybe you’re doing all right. But completely better from a depressed period before this one… I can’t really say that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it more than one, no. It doesn’t feel like separate chunks to me, like one ended and then another started.\n\nIt’s more like… when you mop a hallway and there’s still that dull patch that never really dries right, and you keep noticing it every time you walk by. I’ve had better days mixed in, but I can’t really sort it into “this was one depressed time” and “that was another.” It just feels like this same stretch, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any periods of feeling depressed that completely went away before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’m trying to think of it the way you’re asking, like there was a depressed time and then it went away clean before now. I can’t really put my finger on anything like that.\n\nThere were better days, sure. Sometimes if the building was quiet and I got all my work done, I’d think, okay, maybe I’m doing alright. But then it’s like the same heaviness is still there underneath. So no, I don’t think I had a period where it completely went away.\n\nDoctor: Did you have an episode of depression that completely went away before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: No, not completely. It’s been more like the same thing carrying on.\n\nThe depression part… I’d say it’s been with me about a year and two months now, starting not long after the move. I remember because after moving, there were all these boxes and little things to fix, and I kept thinking once things settled down I’d feel normal again, but it didn’t really lift all the way. So no, I don’t think there was an episode that went away before this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode of depression like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say there was one before, like a separate “episode.” I’ve had rough patches in life, sure, but everybody has those, right? Like when my hours got shifted at work once and I was getting home when the hallway lights were already buzzing—those lights, I swear, they make the whole place feel tired.\n\nBut like this? I don’t know. I can’t point to an earlier time and say, yes, that was the same thing. This feels like it’s been its own long stretch.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression like this before that completely went away on their own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like that, no.\n\nI mean, there were times I probably looked down or felt worn out, but it’s hard to separate that from regular life stuff. Work, getting older, the apartment needing things fixed—there’s always something. But an episode like this that came and then just went away on its own? I can’t really say I’ve had that before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I can’t really say I’ve had that before.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you have any periods of feeling depressed before the one that started after you left your old apartment that were similar to how you're feeling now?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t really know. I mean, before that apartment… there were bad weeks, sure, everybody gets those. I remember one winter the boiler in the building kept acting up and I was dragging myself around with buckets and mop heads, and the whole place smelled like wet cardboard for days.

But similar to this? I can’t say for sure. Nothing really stands out to me as the same kind of thing.

Those carts, if the wheel’s bad, they pull to one side—anyway, that’s what it feels like in me. Like everything takes too much just to begin.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About a year and… five months, I think. It kind of started after I had to leave my old apartment, my old neighborhood. I still think about that place—the corner store had this bell on the door, real loud, you could hear it from the sidewalk sometimes. But yeah… since then it’s been building, not all at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities or hobbies that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I used to look forward to Saturdays because I’d go fishing, even if I didn’t catch anything—there was this one spot where the reeds always got tangled in the line, drove me crazy, but I liked being out there.\n\nAnd I used to do crosswords with coffee, too. Morning paper, same mug… or at work I’d show the younger guys little tricks, like how to strip a floor without making a mess of the edges. Now I mostly just let all that sit. It doesn’t really give me anything back anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I’ve had thoughts like that. Not like I’ve got some big plan written down or anything. It’s more that a lot of mornings I wake up and think, maybe it’d be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all.\n\nMornings are strange that way—the light comes in kind of gray through the blinds, and there’s that few seconds before you remember what day it is. I used to get right up because the building floors don’t clean themselves, you know… but now sometimes I just lie there thinking that. And it scares me enough that I guess I should say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge?\n\nPatient: Nervous or on edge… I’m not sure. I mean, I get uneasy sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s “often” like you mean.\n\nLike, the other day I kept hearing the radiator knock in the hallway—old buildings do that, especially when the heat kicks on—and I caught myself just sitting there listening to it longer than made sense. But I don’t know if that’s nerves or just… being worn out. So, yeah, I’m not really sure how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious… I don’t know. I mean, I know what people mean when they say it, like your stomach twists up or you’re waiting for bad news, but I don’t know if that’s what I’m having.\n\nSometimes I’m just sitting there and I feel kind of stirred up, but then I’ll notice the sink dripping or the hallway light buzzing, and I start thinking about how many times I used to change those bulbs at work because somebody always put in the cheap ones… so I don’t know. I haven’t really kept track of it as anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, but my work pants have been… kind of hanging off me more than they used to.\n\nFood just doesn’t taste right most days. Like cardboard, almost. I’ll make something and then just look at it, or I’ll skip it and end up eating a few crackers after my shift. Those vending machine crackers, the stale kind—though sometimes they’re the only thing that feels easy. So, yeah… I guess I’ve lost some weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling flat or depressed like this before the one that started after you left your old apartment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was like this before, not in the same way. The flat feeling… the low feeling… that really started not too long after I moved out of the old apartment.\n\nIt’s been going on a while now. I guess about a year and a couple months, if I count it out. Funny how you don’t notice the time passing until you think about rent dates and moving boxes and all that junk you never unpack. But yeah… the depressed part has been around since after the move, about a year and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or depressed before in your life, prior to the episode that started after you left your old apartment?\n\nPatient: Before that? I don’t really think so… not like this.\n\nI mean, I’ve had bad stretches, sure—everybody does. Work gets miserable, bills stack up, pipes burst in the winter and then you’re the one mopping it at 2 a.m.… but this low feeling, the one I’m talking about, that started after I left the old apartment.\n\nIt’s been about… a year and two months now, I think. Not right on moving day, but not long after. That’s the one that’s stuck around.\n\nDoctor: Have you had an episode like this before, where you felt flat and depressed for an extended period, that completely went away before this one started?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I had one that came and went before this. I mean, I’ve had rough times, sure, like when a building changes management and suddenly everybody wants the floors done a different way, and you’re supposed to just know it—but not that same flat, heavy kind of depressed feeling for a long stretch.\n\nThis one’s been with me since not long after I moved out of the old apartment. About a year and two months now, if I’m counting right. It didn’t really clear up and then come back.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in your life where you felt completely back to normal after feeling depressed, before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to a time like that, where I was depressed and then, you know, completely normal again before this.\n\nI had days that were better than other days, sure. Like you get through a shift and the floors actually stay clean for five minutes, or the weather’s decent and you think maybe things aren’t so bad. But as far as a whole period where I was down like this and then it just cleared up? I don’t really remember that happening.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel completely better from a depressed period before this one started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in the way you’re saying, like a whole depressed period and then I was just completely fine after.\n\nI’ve had better days, sure. Sometimes a week goes smoother, you know, the trash rooms aren’t overflowing and nobody’s yelling about streaks on the lobby glass. That can make you feel like maybe you’re doing all right. But completely better from a depressed period before this one… I can’t really say that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it more than one, no. It doesn’t feel like separate chunks to me, like one ended and then another started.\n\nIt’s more like… when you mop a hallway and there’s still that dull patch that never really dries right, and you keep noticing it every time you walk by. But then it’s like the same heaviness is still there underneath. It’s been more like the same thing carrying on.\n\nThe depression part… I’d say it’s been with me about a year and two months now, starting not long after the move. pantry is short on supplies or a hallway gets missed, somehow that’s on me too.\n- key: S032\n  name: Death_Suicide\n  description: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a eating, feeling guilty, and thinking I might not want to wake up—I was also hearing that man’s voice at work and believing people in the building were watching me.\n- key: additional_requirement_2\n For most of the time since the move, the sadness, low energy, guilt, and loss of interest have been there in the background, even on days when the voices or camera worries were the main thing on my mind.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: There was a stretch of several weeks when my mood had lifted some and I was getting through meals and work a little better, but I still heard my name in the stairwell and stayed convinced the hallway cameras were being used to track me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: My supervisor started writing up missed routes and repeated cleaning mistakes, and I stopped keeping up with church, fishing, and the people I used to check in on. I don’t know. I can’t point to an earlier time and say, yes, that was the same thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I don’t feel like myself anymore. I mean, I came in because I’m just kind of flat and quiet most of the time now. I don’t talk to people much, even at work, and I used to at least say a few things, you know, about the floors or the weather or whatever.\n\nAnd getting started on my cleaning route… it feels like pushing one of those loaded carts uphill. Those carts, if the wheel’s bad, they pull to one side—anyway, that’s what it feels like in me. Like everything takes too much just to begin.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About a year and… five months, I think. It kind of started after I had to leave my old apartment, my old neighborhood. I still think about that place—the corner store had this bell on the door, real loud, you could hear it from the sidewalk sometimes. But yeah… since then it’s been building, not all at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities or hobbies that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I used to look forward to Saturdays because I’d go fishing, even if I didn’t catch anything—there was this one spot where the reeds always got tangled in the line, drove me crazy, but I liked being out there.\n\nAnd I used to do crosswords with coffee, too. Morning paper, same mug… or at work I’d show the younger guys little tricks, like how to strip a floor without making a mess of the edges. Now I mostly just let all that sit. It doesn’t really give me anything back anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: I… yeah, I’ve had thoughts like that. Not like I’ve got some big plan written down or anything. It’s more that a lot of mornings I wake up and think, maybe it’d be easier if I just didn’t wake up at all.\n\nMornings are strange that way—the light comes in kind of gray through the blinds, and there’s that few seconds before you remember what day it is. I used to get right up because the building floors don’t clean themselves, you know… but now sometimes I just lie there thinking that. And it scares me enough that I guess I should say it out loud.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel nervous or on edge?\n\nPatient: Nervous or on edge… I’m not sure. I mean, I get uneasy sometimes, but I don’t know if it’s “often” like you mean.\n\nLike, the other day I kept hearing the radiator knock in the hallway—old buildings do that, especially when the heat kicks on—and I caught myself just sitting there listening to it longer than made sense. But I don’t know if that’s nerves or just… being worn out. So, yeah, I’m not really sure how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious?\n\nPatient: Anxious… I don’t know. I mean, I know what people mean when they say it, like your stomach twists up or you’re waiting for bad news, but I don’t know if that’s what I’m having.\n\nSometimes I’m just sitting there and I feel kind of stirred up, but then I’ll notice the sink dripping or the hallway light buzzing, and I start thinking about how many times I used to change those bulbs at work because somebody always put in the cheap ones… so I don’t know. I haven’t really kept track of it as anxiety.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I don’t weigh myself much, but my work pants have been… kind of hanging off me more than they used to.\n\nFood just doesn’t taste right most days. Like cardboard, almost. I’ll make something and then just look at it, or I’ll skip it and end up eating a few crackers after my shift. Those vending machine crackers, the stale kind—though sometimes they’re the only thing that feels easy. So, yeah… I guess I’ve lost some weight.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods of feeling flat or depressed like this before the one that started after you left your old apartment?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was like this before, not in the same way. The flat feeling… the low feeling… that really started not too long after I moved out of the old apartment.\n\nIt’s been going on a while now. I guess about a year and a couple months, if I count it out. Funny how you don’t notice the time passing until you think about rent dates and moving boxes and all that junk you never unpack. But yeah… the depressed part has been around since after the move, about a year and two months.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or depressed before in your life, prior to the episode that started after you left your old apartment?\n\nPatient: Before that? I don’t really think so… not like this.\n\nI mean, I’ve had bad stretches, sure—everybody does. Work gets miserable, bills stack up, pipes burst in the winter and then you’re the one mopping it at 2 a.m.… but this low feeling, the one I’m talking about, that started after I left the old apartment.\n\nIt’s been about… a year and two months now, I think. Not right on moving day, but not long after. That’s the one that’s stuck around.\n\nDoctor: Have you had an episode like this before, where you felt flat and depressed for an extended period, that completely went away before this one started?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I had one that came and went before this. I mean, I’ve had rough times, sure, like when a building changes management and suddenly everybody wants the floors done a different way, and you’re supposed to just know it—but not that same flat, heavy kind of depressed feeling for a long stretch.\n\nThis one’s been with me since not long after I moved out of the old apartment. About a year and two months now, if I’m counting right. It didn’t really clear up and then come back.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods in your life where you felt completely back to normal after feeling depressed, before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to a time like that, where I was depressed and then, you know, completely normal again before this.\n\nI had days that were better than other days, sure. Like you get through a shift and the floors actually stay clean for five minutes, or the weather’s decent and you think maybe things aren’t so bad. But as far as a whole period where I was down like this and then it just cleared up? I don’t really remember that happening.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel completely better from a depressed period before this one started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not in the way you’re saying, like a whole depressed period and then I was just completely fine after.\n\nI’ve had better days, sure. Sometimes a week goes smoother, you know, the trash rooms aren’t overflowing and nobody’s yelling about streaks on the lobby glass. That can make you feel like maybe you’re doing all right. But completely better from a depressed period before this one… I can’t really say that happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it more than one, no. It doesn’t feel like separate chunks to me, like one ended and then another started.\n\nIt’s more like… when you mop a hallway and there’s still that dull patch that never really dries right, and you keep noticing it every time you walk by. I’ve had better days mixed in, but I can’t really sort it into “this was one depressed time” and “that was another.” It just feels like this same stretch, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any periods of feeling depressed that completely went away before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’m trying to think of it the way you’re asking, like there was a depressed time and then it went away clean before now. I can’t really put my finger on anything like that.\n\nThere were better days, sure. Sometimes if the building was quiet and I got all my work done, I’d think, okay, maybe I’m doing alright. But then it’s like the same heaviness is still there underneath. So no, I don’t think I had a period where it completely went away.\n\nDoctor: Did you have an episode of depression that completely went away before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: No, not completely. It’s been more like the same thing carrying on.\n\nThe depression part… I’d say it’s been with me about a year and two months now, starting not long after the move. I remember because after moving, there were all these boxes and little things to fix, and I kept thinking once things settled down I’d feel normal again, but it didn’t really lift all the way. So no, I don’t think there was an episode that went away before this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode of depression like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say there was one before, like a separate “episode.” I’ve had rough patches in life, sure, but everybody has those, right? I’ve had better days mixed in, but I can’t really sort it into “this was one depressed time” and “that was another.” It just feels like this same stretch, mostly.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any periods of feeling depressed that completely went away before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’m trying to think of it the way you’re asking, like there was a depressed time and then it went away clean before now. I can’t really put my finger on anything like that.\n\nThere were better days, sure. Sometimes if the building was quiet and I got all my work done, I’d think, okay, maybe I’m doing alright. But then it’s like the same heaviness is still there underneath. So no, I don’t think I had a period where it completely went away.\n\nDoctor: Did you have an episode of depression that completely went away before this current episode started?\n\nPatient: No, not completely. It’s been more like the same thing carrying on.\n\nThe depression part… I’d say it’s been with me about a year and two months now, starting not long after the move. I remember because after moving, there were all these boxes and little things to fix, and I kept thinking once things settled down I’d feel normal again, but it didn’t really lift all the way. So no, I don’t think there was an episode that went away before this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode of depression like this before in your life?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say there was one before, like a separate “episode.” I’ve had rough patches in life, sure, but everybody has those, right? Like when my hours got shifted at work once and I was getting home when the hallway lights were already buzzing—those lights, I swear, they make the whole place feel tired.\n\nBut like this? I don’t know. I can’t point to an earlier time and say, yes, that was the same thing. This feels like it’s been its own long stretch.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any episodes of depression like this before that completely went away on their own?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like that, no.\n\nI mean, there were times I probably looked down or felt worn out, but it’s hard to separate that from regular life stuff. Work, getting older, the apartment needing things fixed—there’s always something. But an episode like this that came and then just went away on its own? I can’t really say I’ve had that before.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any periods of feeling depressed before the one that started after you left your old apartment that were similar to how you're feeling now?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, before that apartment… there were bad weeks, sure, everybody gets those. I remember one winter the boiler in the building kept acting up and I was dragging myself around with buckets and mop heads, and the whole place smelled like wet cardboard for days.\n\nBut similar to this? I can’t say for sure. Nothing really stands out to me as the same kind of thing.\n\nDoctor: Is this the first time in your life you've felt this depressed, or have you felt this way before?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. It feels like the worst one I can remember, but memory gets funny, you know? Like I’ll remember the smell of the floor wax at a building from years ago, but not what month something happened.\n\nI’ve had down times before, sure, but whether I’ve felt exactly this depressed before… I don’t know that I can say. This feels heavier than what I remember.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
